# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 1 | (R$, R)
The risk  $R$  is the value of a probable average annual loss.For each type of loss which may appear in a wind turbine or in a service, the relevant risk shall be evaluated.The risks to be evaluated in a wind turbine may be as follows:$R_{1}$ : risk of loss of human life;$R_{4}$ : risk of loss of economic value.The risk to be evaluated for a service may be as follows:$R^{\prime}_{4}$  risk of loss of economic value.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 2 | (R$, R)
To evaluate the risks,  $R$ , the relevant risk components (partial risks depending on the source and type of damage) shall be defined and calculated.Each risk,  $R$ , is the sum of its risk components.When calculating a risk, the risk components may be grouped according to the source of damage and the type of damage.Risk components for a wind turbine due to lightning striking the wind turbine are:
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 3
$R_{\mathrm{A}}$ : Component related to injury to persons inside the wind turbines and injury to living beings caused by touch and step voltages in the zones up to  $3\mathrm{m}$  outside the wind turbine tower.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 4
Loss of type L1 and in the case of livestock also L4 may arise.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 5
$R_{\mathsf{B}}$  Component related to physical damage caused by dangerous sparking inside the structure triggering fire.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 6
Loss of type L1 and L4 may arise.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 7 | (LEMP.)
$R_{\mathbb{C}}$  Component related to failure of internal systems caused by lightning electromagnetic pulse, LEMP.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 8
Loss of type L4 may arise or L1 in case failure of internal systems immediately endangers human life.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 9
Risk components for a wind turbine due to lightning striking a service connected to the wind turbine are:$R_{\mathsf{U}}$ : Component related to injury to persons caused by touch voltage inside the wind turbine, due to lightning current injected in a line entering the wind turbine.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 10
Loss of type L1 may arise.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 11
$R_{\mathrm{V}}$ : Component related to physical damage (fire triggered by dangerous sparking between external installations and metallic parts generally at the entrance point of the line into the wind turbine) due to a lightning current transmitted through or along the incoming service.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 12
Loss of type L1 and L4 may arise.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 13
$R_{\mathbb{W}}$ : Component related to failure of internal systems caused by overvoltages induced in incoming lines and transmitted to the wind turbine.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 14
Loss of type L4 could occur or L1 in case failure of internal systems immediately endangers human life.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 15
Risk component for a wind turbine due to lightning striking near a service connected to the wind turbine:$R_{Z}$ : Component related to failure of internal systems caused by overvoltages induced in incoming lines and transmitted to the wind turbine.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 16
Loss of type L4 could occur or L1 in case failure of internal systems immediately endangers human life.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 17
Risk components for a service to lightning striking the service connected to the wind turbine are:$R_{\mathrm{V}}^{\prime}$ : Component related to physical damage due to mechanical and thermal effects of lightning current.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 18 | (L'4)
Loss of type L'4 may arise.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 19
$R_{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}$  Component related to failure of lines and connected equipment caused by overvoltages induced on lines.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 20 | (L'4)
Loss of type L'4 may arise.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 21
Risk component for a service due to lightning striking near the service connected to the wind turbine:$R_{Z}^{\prime}$  Component related to failure of lines and connected equipment caused by overvoltages induced on lines.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 22 | (L'4)
Loss of type L'4 could occur.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 23
Risk components for a service due to lightning striking the wind turbine to which the service is connected are:$R_{\text{B}}^{\prime}$ : Component related to physical damage due to mechanical and thermal effects of lightning current flowing along the line.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 24 | (L'4)
Loss of type L'4 may arise.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 25
$R_{\mathrm{C}}^{\prime}$  Component related to failure of connected equipment due to overvoltages by resistive coupling.
----------
# B.2.2 Risk and risk components|Part 26 | (L'4)
Loss of type L'4 may arise.
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine

Risk components to be considered for each type of loss in a wind turbine are listed below:
$R_{1}$ : Risk of loss of human life:
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine|Math Block
$$ 
R _ {1} = R _ {\mathrm {A}} + R _ {\mathrm {B}} + R _ {\mathrm {C}} ^ {3)} + R _ {\mathrm {U}} + R _ {\mathrm {V}} + R _ {\mathrm {W}} ^ {3)} + R _ {\mathrm {Z}} ^ {3)} \tag {B.1}
 $$
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine

$R_{4}$ : Risk of loss of economic value:
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine|Math Block
$$ 
R _ {4} = R _ {\mathrm {A}} ^ {4)} + R _ {\mathrm {B}} + R _ {\mathrm {C}} + R _ {\mathrm {U}} ^ {4)} + R _ {\mathrm {V}} + R _ {\mathrm {W}} + R _ {\mathrm {Z}} \tag {B.2}
 $$
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine

Composition of risk components with reference to the source of damage:
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine|Math Block
$$ 
R = R _ {\mathrm {D}} + R _ {\mathrm {I}} \tag {B.3}
 $$
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine

where
$R_{\mathrm{D}}$  is the risk due to lightning striking the wind turbine (source S1) which is defined as the sum:
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine|Math Block
$$ 
R _ {\mathrm {D}} = R _ {\mathrm {A}} + R _ {\mathrm {B}} + R _ {\mathrm {C}} \tag {B.4}
 $$
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine

$R_{1}$  is the risk due to lightning influencing the wind turbine but not striking the wind turbine (sources: S3 and S4) which is defined as the sum:
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine|Math Block
$$ 
R _ {1} = R _ {\cup} + R _ {\vee} + R _ {\mathbb {W}} + R _ {\mathbb {Z}} \tag {B.5}
 $$
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine

Composition of risk components with reference to type of damage:
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine|Math Block
$$ 
R = R _ {\mathrm {S}} + R _ {\mathrm {F}} + R _ {\mathrm {O}} \tag {B.6}
 $$
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine

where
$R_{\mathbb{S}}$  is the risk of injury to living beings (D1) which is defined as the sum:
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine|Math Block
$$ 
R _ {\mathrm {S}} = R _ {\mathrm {A}} + R _ {\mathrm {U}} \tag {B.7}
 $$
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine

$R_{\mathsf{F}}$  is the risk of physical damage (D2) which is defined as the sum:
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine|Math Block
$$ 
R _ {\mathrm {F}} = R _ {\mathrm {B}} + R _ {\mathrm {V}} \tag {B.8}
 $$
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine

$R_{\mathrm{O}}$  is the risk due to failure of internal systems (D3) which is defined as the sum:
----------
# B.2.3 Composition of risk components related to a wind turbine|Math Block
$$ 
R _ {\mathrm {O}} = R _ {\mathrm {C}} + R _ {\mathrm {W}} + R _ {\mathrm {Z}} \tag {B.9}
 $$
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service

Risk components to be considered for each type of loss in a service line are listed below.
$R_{4}^{\prime}$  : risk of loss of economic value:
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service|Math Block
$$ 
R _ {4} ^ {\prime} = R _ {V} ^ {\prime} + R _ {W} ^ {\prime} + R _ {Z} ^ {\prime} + R _ {B} ^ {\prime} + R _ {C} ^ {\prime} \tag {B.10}
 $$
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service

Composition of risk components with reference to source of damage:
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service|Math Block
$$ 
R ^ {\prime} = R _ {D} ^ {\prime} + R _ {I} ^ {\prime} \tag {B.11}
 $$
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service

where
$R_{\mathsf{D}}^{\prime}$  is the risk due to flashes striking the service line (source S3); defined as the sum:
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service|Math Block
$$ 
R _ {D} ^ {\prime} = R _ {V} ^ {\prime} + R _ {W} ^ {\prime} \tag {B.12}
 $$
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service

$R_{1}^{\prime}$  is the risk due to flashes influencing the service line without striking it (sources S1 and S4); defined as the sum:
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service|Math Block
$$ 
R _ {\mathrm {I}} ^ {\prime} = R _ {\mathrm {B}} ^ {\prime} + R _ {\mathrm {C}} ^ {\prime} + R _ {\mathrm {Z}} ^ {\prime} \tag {B.13}
 $$
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service

Composition of risk components with reference to the type of damage:
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service|Math Block
$$ 
R ^ {\prime} = R _ {F} ^ {\prime} + R _ {O} ^ {\prime} \tag {B.14}
 $$
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service

where
$R_{\mathsf{F}}^{\prime}$  is the risk due to physical damage (D2); defined as the sum:
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service|Math Block
$$ 
R _ {F} ^ {\prime} = R _ {V} ^ {\prime} + R _ {B} ^ {\prime} \tag {B.15}
 $$
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service

$R_{\mathrm{O}}^{\prime}$  is the risk due to failure of internal systems (D3); defined as the sum:
----------
# B.2.4 Composition of risk components related to a service|Math Block
$$ 
R _ {\mathrm {O}} ^ {\prime} = R _ {\mathrm {W}} ^ {\prime} + R _ {\mathrm {Z}} ^ {\prime} + R _ {\mathrm {C}} ^ {\prime} \tag {B.16}
 $$
----------
# B.3.1 Probability,  $P_{\mathbf{A}}$ , that a lightning flash to the wind turbine will cause injury to living beings|html_block
The values of probability,  $P_{\mathrm{A}}$ , of shock to living beings due to touch and step voltage from a lightning flash to the structure (i.e. the wind turbine), as a function of typical protection measures are as given in Table B.3. If more than one of the protection measures is taken, the value of  $P_{\mathrm{A}}$  is the product of the corresponding  $P_{\mathrm{A}}$  values.
Table B.3 - Values of probability,  $P_A$ , that a lightning flash to a wind turbine will cause shock to living beings due to dangerous touch and step voltages (corresponds to Table B.1 in IEC 62305-2)
<table><tr><td>Protection measure</td><td>PA</td><td>Comments</td></tr><tr><td>No protection measures</td><td>1</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Electrical insulation of exposed down-conductor (e.g. at least 3mm cross-linked polyethylene)</td><td>10-2</td><td>Not relevant for wind turbines using the tower structure as down conductor.</td></tr><tr><td>Effective soil equipotentialisation</td><td>10-2</td><td>Mandatory for wind turbines holding HV equipment according to typical electrical codes.</td></tr><tr><td>Warning notices</td><td>10-1</td><td></td></tr></table>
----------
# B.3.2 Probability,  $P_{\mathbb{B}}$ , that a lightning flash to the wind turbine will cause physical damage|html_block
The values of probability,  $P_{\mathrm{B}}$ , of physical damage caused by a lightning flash to the wind turbine as a function of lightning protection level (LPL) are given in Table B.4.
Table B.4 - Values of probability,  $P_{\mathrm{B}}$ , depending on the protection measures to reduce physical damage (corresponds to Table B.2 in IEC 62305-2)
<table><tr><td>Characteristics of wind turbine</td><td>Class of LPS</td><td>PB</td></tr><tr><td>Wind turbine not protected by LPS</td><td>-</td><td>1</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="4">Wind turbine protected by LPS</td><td>IV</td><td>0,2</td></tr><tr><td>III</td><td>0,1</td></tr><tr><td>II</td><td>0,05</td></tr><tr><td>I</td><td>0,02</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Wind turbine with lightning protection of blades and nacelle conforming to LPS I and the tower acting as a continuous natural down conductor</td><td>0,01</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Wind turbine with lightning protection of blades, metal roof nacelle (or equivalent metal mesh) with complete protection of any nacelle roof installations against direct lightning attachment and the tower acting as a continuous natural down conductor</td><td>0,001</td></tr></table>
----------
# B.3.2 Probability,  $P_{\mathbb{B}}$ , that a lightning flash to the wind turbine will cause physical damage | (Table B.4, IEC)

NOTE Values of  $P_{\mathrm{B}}$  other than those in Table B.4 are possible if based on a detailed investigation - refer to IEC 62305-2, Clause B.2.
----------
# B.3.3 Probability,  $P_{\mathbb{C}}$ , that a lightning flash to the wind turbine will cause failure of internal systems | (SPD)

The values of probability,  $P_{\mathrm{C}}$ , of failure of internal systems caused by a lightning flash to the wind turbine depend on the adopted coordinated SPD protection:
----------
# B.3.3 Probability,  $P_{\mathbb{C}}$ , that a lightning flash to the wind turbine will cause failure of internal systems|Math Block
$$ 
P _ {\mathrm {C}} = P _ {\mathrm {S P D}} \tag {B.17}
 $$
----------
# B.3.3 Probability,  $P_{\mathbb{C}}$ , that a lightning flash to the wind turbine will cause failure of internal systems|html_block
Values of  $P_{\text{SPD}}$  depend on the lightning protection level (LPL) for which the SPDs are designed as shown in Table B.5.
Table B.5 - Values of probability  $P_{\mathsf{SPD}}$  as a function of the LPL for which the SPDs are designed (Table B.3 in IEC 62305-2)
<table><tr><td>LPL</td><td>\(P_{\text{SPD}}\)</td></tr><tr><td>No coordinated SPD protection</td><td>1</td></tr><tr><td>III-IV</td><td>0,03</td></tr><tr><td>II</td><td>0,02</td></tr><tr><td>I</td><td>0,01</td></tr><tr><td>See Note 3</td><td>0,005 to 0,001</td></tr></table>
----------
# B.3.3 Probability,  $P_{\mathbb{C}}$ , that a lightning flash to the wind turbine will cause failure of internal systems|Part 1 | (IEC, Coordinated, SPD, LPS, SPD)
NOTE 1 Only "coordinated SPD protection" is suitable as a protection measure to reduce  $P_{\mathrm{C}}$ : Coordinated SPD protection is effective to reduce  $P_{\mathrm{C}}$  only if the wind turbine hub, nacelle and tower are protected with an LPS, or if the structures with continuous metal or reinforced concrete framework act as a natural LPS where bonding and earthing requirements of IEC 62305-3 are satisfied.
----------
# B.3.3 Probability,  $P_{\mathbb{C}}$ , that a lightning flash to the wind turbine will cause failure of internal systems|Part 2 | (SPDs, SPDs)
NOTE 2 Shielding internal systems connected to external lines consisting of lightning protective cable or systems with wiring in lightning protective cable ducts, metallic conduit or metallic tubes; may not require the use of coordinated protection.NOTE 3 Smaller values of  $P_{\mathrm{SPD}}$  are possible in case SPDs have better protection characteristics (higher current withstand capability, lower protective levels, etc.)
----------
# B.3.3 Probability,  $P_{\mathbb{C}}$ , that a lightning flash to the wind turbine will cause failure of internal systems|Part 3 | (LPL)
compared with the requirements defined for LPL I at the relevant installation locations.
----------
# B.3.4 Probability,  $P_{\mathsf{M}}$ , that a lightning flash near the wind turbine will cause failure of internal systems|Part 1
Due to the height of wind turbines, most lightning flashes will strike the turbines directly and not in the area near the wind turbine.
----------
# B.3.4 Probability,  $P_{\mathsf{M}}$ , that a lightning flash near the wind turbine will cause failure of internal systems|Part 2
Furthermore, the large metal structures will shield the internal systems.
----------
# B.3.4 Probability,  $P_{\mathsf{M}}$ , that a lightning flash near the wind turbine will cause failure of internal systems|Part 3 | (IEC)
Hence the probability that a lightning flash near the wind turbine will cause failure of internal systems can be considered negligible when the wind turbine hub, nacelle and tower are protected with an LPS or when the structures with continuous metal or reinforced concrete framework act as a natural LPS where bonding and earthing requirements of IEC 62305-3 are satisfied.
----------
# B.3.5 Probability,  $P_{\mathsf{U}}$ , that a lightning flash to a service line will cause injury to living beings|html_block
The values of probability,  $P_{\mathrm{U}}$ , of injury to living beings due to touch voltage caused by lightning flashes to a service line (power cable or communication cable) entering the wind turbine depends on the characteristics of the service line shield, the impulse withstand voltage of internal systems connected to the service line, the typical protection measures (physical restrictions, warning notices, etc. (see Table B.3) and the SPDs provided at the entrance of the service line.
When SPDs are not provided for equipotential bonding in accordance with IEC 62305-3, the value of  $P_{\mathrm{U}}$  is equal to the value of  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$ , where  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$  is the probability of failure of internal systems due to a lightning flash to the connected service line.
Values of  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$  are given in Table B.6.
When SPDs are provided for equipotential bonding in accordance with IEC 62305-3, the value of  $P_{\mathrm{U}}$  is the lower value between  $P_{\mathrm{SPD}}$  (Table B.5) and  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$ .
NOTE Coordinated SPD protection according to IEC 62305-4 is not necessary to reduce  $P_{\mathsf{U}}$  in this case. SPDs according to IEC 62305-3 are sufficient.
Table B.6 - Values of probability,  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$ , depending on the resistance,  $R_{\mathrm{S}}$ , of the cable screen and the impulse withstand voltage,  $U_{\mathrm{W}}$ , of the equipment (Table B.6 in IEC 62305-2)
<table><tr><td>Uw
kV</td><td>5 &lt; RS ≤ 20
Ω/km</td><td>1 &lt; RS ≤ 5
Ω/km</td><td>RS ≤ 1
Ω/km</td></tr><tr><td>1,5</td><td>1</td><td>0,8</td><td>0,4</td></tr><tr><td>2,5</td><td>0,95</td><td>0,6</td><td>0,2</td></tr><tr><td>4</td><td>0,9</td><td>0,3</td><td>0,04</td></tr><tr><td>6</td><td>0,8</td><td>0,1</td><td>0,02</td></tr><tr><td colspan="4">RS [Ω/km] is the resistance of the cable shield.</td></tr></table>
----------
# B.3.5 Probability,  $P_{\mathsf{U}}$ , that a lightning flash to a service line will cause injury to living beings | (B.3.)

For unshielded service line,  $P_{\mathrm{LD}} = 1$  shall be taken.
When protection measures such as physical restrictions, warning notices, etc. are provided, the probability,  $P_{\mathrm{U}}$ , shall be further reduced by multiplying it by the values of probability,  $P_{\mathrm{A}}$ , given in Table B.3.
----------
# B.3.6 Probability,  $P_{\mathrm{V}}$ , that a lightning flash to a service line will cause physical damage|Part 1 | (SPDs)
The values of probability,  $P_{\mathrm{V}}$ , of physical damage caused by a lightning flash to a service line entering the wind turbine depend on the characteristics of the service line shield, the impulse withstand voltage of internal systems connected to the service line and the SPDs provided.
----------
# B.3.6 Probability,  $P_{\mathrm{V}}$ , that a lightning flash to a service line will cause physical damage|Part 2 | (SPDs, IEC, Table B.6.)
When SPDs are not provided for equipotential bonding according to IEC 62305-3, the value of  $P_{\mathrm{V}}$  is equal to the values of  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$ , where  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$  is the probability of failure of internal systems due to a flash to the connected service line.Values of  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$  are given in Table B.6.
----------
# B.3.6 Probability,  $P_{\mathrm{V}}$ , that a lightning flash to a service line will cause physical damage|Part 3 | (SPDs, IEC)
When SPDs are provided for equipotential bonding in accordance with IEC 62305-3, the value of  $P_{\mathrm{V}}$  is the lower value between  $P_{\mathrm{SPD}}$  (Table B.5) and  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$ .
----------
# B.3.6 Probability,  $P_{\mathrm{V}}$ , that a lightning flash to a service line will cause physical damage|Part 4 | (IEC, case.SPDs, SPD, 62305-4)
NOTE Coordinated SPD protection according to IEC 62305-4 is not necessary to reduce  $P_{\mathrm{U}}$  in this case.SPDs according to IEC 62305-3 are sufficient.
----------
# B.3.7 Probability,  $P_{\mathbb{W}}$ , that a lightning flash to a service line will cause failure of internal systems|Part 1 | (SPDs)
The values of probability,  $P_{\mathrm{W}}$ , of failure of internal systems caused by lightning flash to a service line entering the wind turbine depend on the characteristics of the service line shielding, the impulse withstand voltage of internal systems connected to the service line and the SPDs provided.
----------
# B.3.7 Probability,  $P_{\mathbb{W}}$ , that a lightning flash to a service line will cause failure of internal systems|Part 2 | (IEC, IEC 62305-4, SPD)
When coordinated SPD protection conforming to IEC 62305-4 is not provided, the value of  $P_{\mathrm{W}}$  is equal to the value of  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$ , where  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$  is the probability of failure of internal systems due to a lightning flash to the connected service line.
----------
# B.3.7 Probability,  $P_{\mathbb{W}}$ , that a lightning flash to a service line will cause failure of internal systems|Part 3
Values of  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$  are given in Table B.6.
----------
# B.3.7 Probability,  $P_{\mathbb{W}}$ , that a lightning flash to a service line will cause failure of internal systems|Part 4 | (IEC, IEC 62305-4, B.5)
When coordinated SPD protection conforming to IEC 62305-4 is provided, the value of  $P_{\mathrm{W}}$  is the lower value between  $P_{\mathrm{SPD}}$  (see Table B.5) and  $P_{\mathrm{LD}}$ .
----------
# B.3.8 Probability,  $P_{\mathbf{Z}}$ , that a lightning flash near an incoming service line will cause failure of internal systems|html_block
The values of probability,  $P_Z$ , that a lightning flash near to a service line entering the structure will cause a failure of internal systems depend on the characteristics of the service line shield, the impulse withstand voltage of the system connected to the service line and protection measures provided.
When coordinated SPD protection conforming to IEC 62305-4 is not provided, the value of  $P_{\mathbb{Z}}$  is equal to the value of  $P_{\mathsf{Ll}}$ , where  $P_{\mathsf{Ll}}$  is the probability of failure of internal systems due to a lightning flash near the connected service line.
Values of  $P_{\mathsf{LI}}$  are given in Table B.7.
When coordinated SPD protection conforming to IEC 62305-4 is provided, the value of  $P_Z$  is the lower value between  $P_{\mathrm{SPD}}$  (see Table B.5) and  $P_{\mathrm{LI}}$ .
Table B.7 - Values of probability,  $P_{\mathrm{LI}}$ , depending on the resistance,  $R_{\mathrm{S}}$ , of the cable screen and the impulse withstand voltage,  $U_{\mathrm{W}}$ , of the equipment (Table B.7 in IEC 62305-2)

# B.4.1 General | (IEC.)

The values of the amount of loss,  $L_{X}$ , should be evaluated and fixed by the lightning protection designer (or the owner of the wind turbine). The typical mean values given here are merely values proposed by the IEC. Different values may be assigned by each national committee (or agreed between purchaser and customer).
----------
# B.4.2 Average relative amount of loss per year

The loss,  $L_{X}$ , refers to the mean relative amount of a particular type of damage which may be caused by a lightning flash considering both its extent and effects.
Its value depends on:
----------
# B.4.2 Average relative amount of loss per year|bullet_list_open
- the number of persons and the time for which they remain in the hazardous place;  
the value of lost production;
- the value of wind turbine components affected by the damage.
----------
# B.4.2 Average relative amount of loss per year

The loss,  $L_{X}$ , varies with the type of loss (L1, L2, L3 and L4) considered and, for each type of loss, with the type of damage (D1, D2 and D3) causing the loss. The following symbols are used:
$L_{\dagger}$  is the loss due to injury from touch and step voltage;  
$L_{\mathrm{f}}$  is the loss due to physical damage;
----------
# B.4.2 Average relative amount of loss per year|bullet_list_open
- $L_{0}$  is the loss due to failure of internal systems.
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life

The value of  $L_{\mathfrak{t}}$ ,  $L_{\mathfrak{f}}$  and  $L_{0}$  may be determined in terms of the relative number of victims from the following approximate relationship:
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {X} = \left(n _ {\mathrm {p}} / n _ {\mathrm {t}}\right) \cdot \left(t _ {\mathrm {p}} / 8 7 6 0\right) \tag {B.18}
 $$
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|Part 1
where
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|Part 2
$n_{\mathfrak{p}}$  is the number of possible endangered persons (victims);$n_{\mathrm{t}}$  is the expected total number of persons (in the wind turbine);$t_{\mathfrak{p}}$  is the time in hours per year for which the persons are present in a dangerous place, outside the wind turbine  $(L_{\mathrm{t}}$  only) or inside the wind turbine  $(L_{\mathrm{t}}, L_{\mathrm{f}}$  and  $L_{0}$ ).
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|Part 3
Loss of human life is affected by the characteristics of the wind turbine structure.
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|Part 4
These are taken into account by increasing  $(h_{\mathsf{Z}})$  and decreasing  $(r_{\mathrm{f}}, r_{\mathrm{p}}, r_{\mathrm{a}}, r_{\mathrm{u}})$  factors as follows:
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {\mathrm {A}} = r _ {\mathrm {a}} \cdot L _ {\mathrm {t}} \tag {B.19}
 $$
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {\mathrm {U}} = r _ {\mathrm {u}} \cdot L _ {\mathrm {t}} \tag {B.20}
 $$
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {\mathrm {B}} = L _ {\mathrm {V}} = r _ {\mathrm {p}} \cdot h _ {\mathrm {Z}} \cdot r _ {\mathrm {f}} \cdot L _ {\mathrm {f}} \tag {B.21}
 $$
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {\mathrm {C}} = L _ {\mathrm {M}} = L _ {\mathrm {W}} = L _ {\mathrm {Z}} = L _ {\mathrm {O}} \tag {B.22}
 $$
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|html_block
where
$L_{\mathrm{A}}$  is loss related to injury to living beings;
$L_{\mathrm{B}}$  is loss in a structure related to physical damage (flashes to structure);
$L_{\mathrm{C}}$  is loss related to failure of internal systems (flashes to service line);
$L_{\mathbb{M}}$  is loss related to failure of internal systems (flashes near structure);
$L_{\mathsf{U}}$  is loss related to injury of living beings (flashes to service line);
$L_{\mathrm{V}}$  is loss in a structure due to physical damage (flashes to service line);
$r_{\mathrm{a}}$  is a factor reducing the loss of human life depending on the type of soil (see Table B.8);
$r_{\mathbf{u}}$  is a factor reducing the loss of human life depending on the type of floor (see Table B.8);
$r_{\mathfrak{p}}$  is a factor reducing the loss due to physical damage depending on the provisions made to reduce the consequences of fire (see Table B.9);
$r_{\mathrm{f}}$  is a factor reducing the loss due to physical damage depending on the risk of fire in the wind turbine (see Table B.10);
$h_Z$  is a factor increasing the loss due to physical damage when a special hazard is present (see Table B.11).
Table B.8 - Values of reduction factors  $r_{\mathrm{a}}$  and  $r_{\mathrm{u}}$  as a function of the type of surface of soil or floor (corresponds to Table C.2 in IEC 62305-2)
<table><tr><td>Type of surface</td><td>Contact resistance kΩa</td><td>raand ru</td></tr><tr><td>Agricultural, concrete</td><td>≤ 1</td><td>10-2</td></tr><tr><td>Marble, ceramic</td><td>1 to 10</td><td>10-3</td></tr><tr><td>Gravel</td><td>10 to 100</td><td>10-4</td></tr><tr><td>Asphalt, wood</td><td>≥ 100</td><td>10-5</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3">a Values measured between a 400 cm2 electrode compressed with force of 500 N at a point of infinity.</td></tr></table>
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|html_block
Table B.9 - Values of reduction factor  $r_p$  as a function of provisions taken to reduce the consequences of fire (Table C.3 in IEC 62305-2)
<table><tr><td>Provisions</td><td>\(r_p\)</td></tr><tr><td>No provisions</td><td>1</td></tr><tr><td>One of the following provisions: extinguishers; fixed manually operated extinguishing installations; manual alarm installations; hydrants; fire proof compartments; protected escape routes</td><td>0,5</td></tr><tr><td>One of the following provisions: fixed automatically operated extinguishing installations; automatic alarm installations\(^a\)</td><td>0,2</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">\(^a\) Only if protected against overvoltages and other damage and if firemen can arrive within less than 10 min.</td></tr></table>
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|html_block
If more than one provision has been taken, the value of  $r_{\mathfrak{p}}$  shall be taken as the lowest of the relevant values.
NOTE 1 Risk of explosion is not considered relevant for wind turbines.
Table B.10 - Values of reduction factor  $r_{\mathrm{f}}$  as a function of risk of fire of the wind turbine (corresponds to Table C.4 in IEC 62305-2)
<table><tr><td>Risk of fire</td><td>rf</td></tr><tr><td>High</td><td>10-1</td></tr><tr><td>Ordinary</td><td>10-2</td></tr><tr><td>Low</td><td>10-3</td></tr><tr><td>None</td><td>0</td></tr></table>
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life|html_block
NOTE 2 Structures considered as having a high risk of fire may be assumed to be structures with surface materials (blades and nacelle roofs) made of combustible materials with a specific fire load larger than  $800\mathrm{MJ} / \mathrm{m}^2$  
NOTE 3 Structures considered as having an ordinary risk of fire may be assumed to be structures with surface materials (blades and nacelle roofs) made of combustible materials with a specific fire load between  $800\mathrm{MJ} / \mathrm{m}^2$  and  $400\mathrm{MJ} / \mathrm{m}^2$ .  
NOTE 4 Structures considered as having a low risk of fire may be assumed to be structures with surface materials (blades and nacelle roofs) made of combustible materials with a specific fire load less than  $400\mathrm{MJ} / \mathrm{m}^2$  
NOTE 5 Specific fire load is the ratio of the energy of the total amount of the combustible material in a structure and the overall surface of the structure.
Table B.11 – Values of factor  $h_{z}$  increasing the relative amount of loss in presence of a special hazard (corresponds to Table C.5 in IEC 62305-2)
<table><tr><td>Kind of special hazard</td><td>hZ</td></tr><tr><td>No special hazard</td><td>1</td></tr><tr><td>Low level of panic (few and professional persons)</td><td>2</td></tr><tr><td>Difficulty of evacuation</td><td>5</td></tr></table>
----------
# B.4.3 Loss of human life

NOTE 6 Loss of service to the public is not considered relevant for wind turbines, as loss of revenue from produced electricity should be considered economically only.
NOTE 7 Loss of irreplaceable cultural heritage is not considered relevant for wind turbines.
----------
# B.4.4 Economic loss

The value of  $L_{\mathbf{t}}$ ,  $L_{\mathbf{f}}$  and  $L_{0}$  can be determined in terms of the relative amount of possible loss from the following approximate relationship:
----------
# B.4.4 Economic loss|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {X} = c / c _ {\mathrm {t}} \tag {B.23}
 $$
----------
# B.4.4 Economic loss|html_block
where
$c$  is the mean value of possible loss of the wind turbine (including its content, earning of revenue and consequences) in currency;  
$c_{\mathfrak{t}}$  is the total value of the wind turbine (including its content and earning of revenue) in currency.
Typical mean values of  $L_{\mathrm{t}}$ ,  $L_{\mathrm{f}}$  and  $L_{0}$  for use when the determination of  $c$  and  $c_{\mathrm{t}}$  is uncertain or difficult are given in Table B.12.
Table B.12 - Typical mean values of  ${L}_{\mathrm{t}},{L}_{\mathrm{f}}$  and  ${L}_{\mathrm{o}}$  (corresponds to Table C.7 in IEC 62305-2)
<table><tr><td>Wind turbine</td><td>Value</td></tr><tr><td>LtInside</td><td>10-4</td></tr><tr><td>LtOutside</td><td>10-2</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Lf</td><td>10-1</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>L0</td><td>10-4</td></tr></table>

# B.4.4 Economic loss

Loss of economical value is affected by the characteristics of the structure. These are taken into account by increasing  $(h_{\mathsf{Z}})$  and decreasing  $(r_{\mathsf{p}}, r_{\mathsf{a}}, r_{\mathsf{f}}, r_{\mathsf{u}})$  factors as follows:
----------
# B.4.4 Economic loss|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {\mathrm {A}} = r _ {\mathrm {a}} \cdot L _ {\mathrm {t}} \tag {B.24}
 $$
----------
# B.4.4 Economic loss|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {\mathrm {U}} = r _ {\mathrm {u}} \cdot L _ {\mathrm {t}} \tag {B.25}
 $$
----------
# B.4.4 Economic loss|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {\mathrm {B}} = L _ {\mathrm {V}} = r _ {\mathrm {p}} \cdot h _ {\mathrm {Z}} \cdot r _ {\mathrm {f}} \cdot L _ {\mathrm {f}} \tag {B.26}
 $$
----------
# B.4.4 Economic loss|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {\mathrm {C}} = L _ {\mathrm {M}} = L _ {\mathrm {W}} = L _ {\mathrm {Z}} = L _ {\mathrm {O}} \tag {B.27}
 $$
----------
# where|Part 1
$L_{\mathsf{A}}$  is loss related to injury to living beings;
----------
# where|Part 2
$L_{\mathrm{B}}$  is loss in a structure related to physical damage (flashes to structure);$L_{\mathrm{C}}$  is loss related to failure of internal systems (flashes to service line);$L_{\mathbb{M}}$  is loss related to failure of internal systems (flashes near structure);
----------
# where|Part 3
$L_{\mathsf{U}}$  is loss related to injury of living beings (flashes to service line);
----------
# where|Part 4
$L_{\mathrm{V}}$  is loss in a structure due to physical damage (flashes to service line);
----------
# where|Part 5 | (Table B.9, B.8)
$r_{\mathrm{a}}$  is a factor reducing the loss of human life depending on the type of soil (see Table B.8);$r_{\mathbf{u}}$  is a factor reducing the loss of human life depending on the type of floor (see Table B.8);$r_{\mathfrak{p}}$  is a factor reducing the loss due to physical damage depending on the provisions made to reduce the consequences of fire (see Table B.9);
----------
# where|Part 6 | (B.11)
$r_{\mathrm{f}}$  is a factor reducing the loss due to physical damage depending on the risk of fire in the wind turbine (see Table B.10);$h_Z$  is a factor increasing the loss due to physical damage when a special hazard is present (see Table B.11).
----------
# B.5.1.1 Probability  $P_{\mathsf{B}}'$  and  $P_{\mathsf{C}}'$  that a flash to the wind turbine to which a service line is connected will cause damage|Part 1
The probability  $P_{\mathrm{B}}'$  that a flash to a wind turbine to which a service line is connected will cause physical damages, and the probability  $P_{\mathrm{C}}'$  that a flash to the wind turbine to which the service line is connected will cause failures of the service line equipment are related to the failure current  $I_{\mathrm{a}}$ .
----------
# B.5.1.1 Probability  $P_{\mathsf{B}}'$  and  $P_{\mathsf{C}}'$  that a flash to the wind turbine to which a service line is connected will cause damage|Part 2
$I_{\mathrm{a}}$  is dependent on the characteristics of the service line, the number of incoming service lines to the wind turbine and the adopted protection measures.
----------
# B.5.1.1 Probability  $P_{\mathsf{B}}'$  and  $P_{\mathsf{C}}'$  that a flash to the wind turbine to which a service line is connected will cause damage|Part 3
For unshielded service lines,  $I_{\mathrm{a}} = 0$  kA must be assumed.For shielded service lines, the failure current  $I_{\mathrm{a}}(\mathrm{kA})$  shall be evaluated according to:
----------
# B.5.1.1 Probability  $P_{\mathsf{B}}'$  and  $P_{\mathsf{C}}'$  that a flash to the wind turbine to which a service line is connected will cause damage|Math Block
$$ 
I _ {a} = 2 5 n \cdot U _ {W} / \left(R _ {s} \cdot K _ {d} \cdot K _ {p}\right) \tag {B.28}
 $$
----------
# B.5.1.1 Probability  $P_{\mathsf{B}}'$  and  $P_{\mathsf{C}}'$  that a flash to the wind turbine to which a service line is connected will cause damage|html_block
where
$K_{\mathrm{d}}$  is a factor depending on characteristics of the service line (see Table B.13);
$K_{\mathfrak{p}}$  is a factor taking into account the effects of adopted protection measures (see Table B.14);
$U_{\mathrm{W}}$  [kV] is the impulse withstand voltage (see Table B.15 for cables and Table B.16 for apparatus);
$R_{\mathrm{S}}$  [Ω/km] is the shield resistance of the cable;
$n$  is the number of service lines incoming to the wind turbine.
NOTE SPDs at entrance point into the wind turbine increase the failure current  $I_{\mathrm{a}}$  and may have a positive protection effect

# B.5.1.2 Probabilities  $P_{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}$  and  $P_{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}$  that a flash to a service line will cause damages|Part 1
The probability  $P_{\mathrm{V}}'$  that a flash to a service line will cause physical damage, and the probability  $P_{\mathrm{W}}'$  that a flash to a service line will cause failure of service line equipment is related to the failure current  $I_{\mathrm{a}}$  which, in turn, depends on the characteristics of the service line and on the protective measures adopted.
----------
# B.5.1.2 Probabilities  $P_{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}$  and  $P_{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}$  that a flash to a service line will cause damages|Part 2
For unshielded service lines  $I_{\mathrm{a}} = 0$  kA must be assumed.For shielded service lines, the failure current  $I_{\mathrm{a}}$  (kA) shall be evaluated according to:
----------
# B.5.1.2 Probabilities  $P_{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}$  and  $P_{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}$  that a flash to a service line will cause damages|Math Block
$$ 
I _ {\mathrm {a} \cdot} = 2 5 \cdot U _ {\mathrm {W}} / \left(R _ {\mathrm {s}} \cdot K _ {\mathrm {d}} \cdot K _ {\mathrm {p}}\right) \tag {B.29}
 $$
----------
# B.5.1.2 Probabilities  $P_{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}$  and  $P_{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}$  that a flash to a service line will cause damages|Part 1
where

# B.5.1.2 Probabilities  $P_{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}$  and  $P_{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}$  that a flash to a service line will cause damages|Part 2 | (B.14, B.15, B.13)
$K_{\mathrm{d}}$  is a factor depending on characteristics of the service line (see Table B.13);$K_{\mathfrak{p}}$  is a factor taking into account the effects of adopted protection measures (see Table B.14);$U_{\mathrm{W}}$  [kV] is the impulse withstand voltage (see Table B.15 for cables and Table B.16 for apparatus);$R_{\mathrm{S}}$ $[\Omega/\mathrm{km}]$  is the shield resistance of the cable.
----------
# B.5.1.2 Probabilities  $P_{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}$  and  $P_{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}$  that a flash to a service line will cause damages|Part 3
When evaluating  $P_{\mathrm{V}}'$  for telecommunication lines, the maximum values of failure current  $I_{\mathrm{a}}$  to be assumed are as follows:$I_{\mathrm{a}} = 40 \mathrm{kA}$  for cables with lead shield;$I_{\mathrm{a}} = 20 \mathrm{kA}$  for cables with an aluminium shield.NOTE These values are a rough estimation of the test current  $(I_{\mathrm{t}})$  damaging typical telecommunication cables at the lightning striking point.
----------
# B.5.1.2 Probabilities  $P_{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}$  and  $P_{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}$  that a flash to a service line will cause damages|Part 4 | (B.17.)
If any evidence exists that these values are not applicable for a given cable design, other values may be used.In this case, tests should be used for the evaluation of the failure current.The values of  $P_{\mathrm{V}}^{\prime}$  and  $P_{\mathrm{W}}^{\prime}$  as a function of values of the failure current  $I_{\mathrm{a}}$  are given in Table B.17.

# B.5.1.3 Probability  $P_{Z}^{\prime}$  that a flash near the service line will cause damage|Part 1
The probability  $P_{\mathsf{Z}}'$  that a flash near the service line will cause failure of connected apparatus depends on the characteristics of the service line and on the protection measures adopted.
----------
# B.5.1.3 Probability  $P_{Z}^{\prime}$  that a flash near the service line will cause damage|Part 2 | (IEC, SPDs, IEC 62305-4, B.5)
When SPDs conforming to IEC 62305-4 are not provided, the value of  $P_{Z}^{\prime}$  is equal to the value of  $P_{\mathrm{LI}}$ .Values of  $P_{\mathrm{LI}}$  are reported in Table B.7.When SPDs conforming to IEC 62305-4 are provided, the values of  $P_{Z}^{\prime}$  are the lower values between  $P_{\mathrm{SPD}}$  (see Table B.5) and  $P_{\mathrm{LI}}$ .

# B.6.1 General

The loss  $L_{\times}^{\prime}$  refers to the mean relative amount of a particular type of damage which may occur as the result of a flash to a service, considering both the extent and consequential effects.
Its value depends on:
----------
# B.6.1 General|bullet_list_open
- the type and importance of the service provided to the public;
- the value of the goods affected by the damage.
----------
# B.6.1 General|Part 1
The loss  $L_{X}^{\prime}$  varies with the type of loss  $(L_{1}^{\prime}, L_{2}^{\prime}$  and  $L_{4}^{\prime}$ ) considered and, for each type of loss, with the type of damage (D2 and D3) causing the loss.
----------
# B.6.1 General|Part 2
The following symbols are used:
----------
# B.6.1 General|Part 3
$L_{f}^{\prime}$  loss due to physical damage;$L_{0}^{\prime}$  loss due to failure of internal systems.NOTE Loss of service to the public is not considered relevant for wind turbines, hence the loss  $L_{\mathsf{X}}'$  in a service is only considered as an economic loss.
----------
# B.6.2 Economic loss

The value of  $L_{\mathrm{f}}'$  and  $L_{\mathrm{o}}'$  can be determined in terms of the relative amount of possible loss from the approximate relationship:
----------
# B.6.2 Economic loss|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {X} ^ {\prime} = c / c _ {t} \tag {B.30}
 $$
----------
# B.6.2 Economic loss

where
$c$  is the mean value of possible loss of the wind turbine, its content and relevant activities, in currency;  
$c_{\mathfrak{t}}$  is the total values of  $L_{\mathrm{f}}^{\prime}$  and  $L_{\mathrm{o}}^{\prime}$ , for use for all types of service lines when the determination of  $c$  and  $c_{\mathfrak{t}}$  is uncertain or difficult, are as follows:
----------
# B.6.2 Economic loss|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {\prime} = 1 0 ^ {- 1}
 $$
----------
# B.6.2 Economic loss|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {\mathrm {o}} ^ {\prime} = 1 0 ^ {- 3}
 $$
----------
# B.6.2 Economic loss

The loss of economic values is affected by service characteristics as follows:
----------
# B.6.2 Economic loss|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {\mathrm {B}} ^ {\prime} = L _ {\mathrm {V}} ^ {\prime} = L _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {\prime} \tag {B.31}
 $$
----------
# B.6.2 Economic loss|Math Block
$$ 
L _ {\mathrm {C}} ^ {\prime} = L _ {\mathrm {W}} ^ {\prime} = L _ {\mathrm {Z}} ^ {\prime} = L _ {\mathrm {o}} ^ {\prime} \tag {B.32}
 $$
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss

The cost of total loss  $C_{\mathrm{L}}$  may be calculated by the following equation:
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss|Math Block
$$ 
C _ {\mathrm {L}} = \left(R _ {\mathrm {A}} + R _ {\mathrm {U}}\right) \cdot C _ {\mathrm {A}} + \left(R _ {\mathrm {B}} + R _ {\mathrm {V}}\right) \cdot \left(C _ {\mathrm {A}} + C _ {\mathrm {B}} + C _ {\mathrm {S}} + C _ {\mathrm {C}}\right) + \left(R _ {\mathrm {C}} + R _ {\mathrm {M}} + R _ {\mathrm {W}} + R _ {\mathrm {Z}}\right) \cdot C _ {\mathrm {S}} \tag {B.33}
 $$
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss|Part 1
where
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss|Part 2
$R_{\mathsf{A}}$  and  $R_{\mathsf{U}}$  are the risk components related to loss of animals, without protection measures;$R_{\mathsf{B}}$  and  $R_{\mathsf{V}}$  are the risk components related to physical damage, without protection measures;$R_{\mathbb{C}}$ ,  $R_{\mathbb{M}}$ ,  $R_{\mathbb{W}}$ ,  $R_{\mathbb{Z}}$  are the risk components related to failure of electrical and electronic systems, without protection measures;$C_{\mathsf{A}}$  is the cost of the animals;
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss|Part 3
$C_{\mathrm{B}}$  is the cost of systems in the wind turbine;$C_{\mathbf{S}}$  is the cost of the wind turbine;$C_{\mathbb{C}}$  is the cost of the contents of the wind turbine.The total cost  $C_{\mathsf{RL}}$  of residual loss in spite of protection measures may be calculated by the means of the formula:
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss|Math Block
$$ 
C _ {\mathrm {R L}} = \left(R _ {\mathrm {A}} ^ {\prime} + R _ {\mathrm {U}} ^ {\prime}\right) \cdot C _ {\mathrm {A}} + \left(R _ {\mathrm {B}} ^ {\prime} + R _ {\mathrm {V}} ^ {\prime}\right) \cdot \left(C _ {\mathrm {A}} + C _ {\mathrm {B}} + C _ {\mathrm {S}} + C _ {\mathrm {C}}\right) + \left(R _ {\mathrm {C}} ^ {\prime} + R _ {\mathrm {M}} ^ {\prime} + R _ {\mathrm {W}} ^ {\prime} + R _ {\mathrm {Z}} ^ {\prime}\right) \cdot C _ {\mathrm {S}} \tag {B.34}
 $$
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss|Part 1
where
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss|Part 2
$R_{\mathsf{A}}^{\prime}$  and  $R_{\mathsf{U}}^{\prime}$  are the risk components related to loss of animals, with protection measures;$R_{\mathsf{B}}^{\prime}$  and  $R_{\mathsf{V}}^{\prime}$  are the risk components related to physical damage, with protection measures;$R_{\mathsf{C}}^{\prime}, R_{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}, R_{\mathsf{W}}^{\prime}, R_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\prime}$  are the risk components related to failure of electrical and electronic systems, with protection measures.
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss|Part 3
The annual cost  $C_{\mathrm{PM}}$  of protection measures may be calculated by means of the equation:
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss|Math Block
$$ 
C _ {\mathrm {P M}} = C _ {\mathrm {P}} \cdot (i + a + m) \tag {B.35}
 $$
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss | (S, S$)

where
$C_{\mathbb{P}}$  is the cost of production measures;
$i$  is the interest rate;
$a$  is the amortisation rate;
$m$  is the maintenance rate.
The annual saving  $S$  of money is:
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss|Math Block
$$ 
S = C _ {\mathrm {L}} - \left(C _ {\mathrm {P M}} + C _ {\mathrm {R L}}\right) \tag {B.36}
 $$
----------
# B.7 Evaluation of costs of loss

Protection is convenient if the annual savings  $S > 0$ .
----------
# B.8 Case studies

Under consideration.
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 1 | (.CFRP)
Modern wind turbine blades are large hollow structures manufactured of composite materials, such as glass fibre reinforced plastic (GFRP), wood, wood laminate and carbon fibre reinforced plastic (CFRP).CFRP is typically used for reinforcement of the blade structure or for special components such as the tip shaft for blades with tip brakes (tip-stall braking mechanism).
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 2 | (case.Lightning)
Some parts and discrete components such as mounting flanges, balancing weights, hinges, bearings, wires, electrical wiring, springs and fixtures are made of metal.At some point in time, hopes were high that lightning would not strike blades made of nonconducting material only, but practical experiences have clearly demonstrated that this is not the case.Lightning does in fact strike blades without any metallic components, and whenever a lightning arc is formed inside the blade damage is severe.
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 3
The two sides or surface skins of a blade are normally manufactured as separate sheets of glass fibre or other composite materials glued together along the leading and trailing edges and to an internal load-carrying structure also made of glass fibre.Inside the blade, there are large air-filled cavities formed by the surface skin and the internal structure and stretching the entire length of the blade.
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 4
Alternatively, the blade skins also supply the mechanical strength of the blade in which the load carrying spar is avoided.Finally, blades may be cast in one piece and hence such blades are without the above-mentioned glued interfaces.There are several types of blades depending on the control and braking mechanism employed, and the use of insulating and conductive composites.Five main types are shown in Figure C.1.
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 5 | (A)
Type A blades use a flap (aileron) in the outer part of the leading edge for braking.On type A blades, lightning attachment points are often found on the steel flap hinges, and severe damage is often seen since the cross section of the steel wires used for operating the flap is usually insufficient for conducting the lightning current.
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 6
![](01K8WFQ2YT/images/8b7a64bee20cba35a36e5df5a253aecd37a66298d65571f02cf2b3fed150c32e.jpg)
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 7
Figure C.1 - Types of wind turbine bladesType B blades use a tip brake which is retained by a spring and released at excessive rotational speed by centrifugal force.With type B blades, lightning attachment points are predominantly seen within a few tens of centimetres from the outermost tip, or on the sides of the tip at the position of the outermost end of the tip shaft.
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 8
From the attachment point, a lightning arc is formed inside the tip section to the outermost end of the tip shaft, and from the other end of the shaft, an arc is formed inside the main blade down to the steel mounting flange at the blade root.Such internal arcs invariably cause catastrophic destruction to the blade.Blades of types A and B were commonly used with older wind turbines as large as  $100\mathrm{kW}$ .Type C is a blade with a tip brake controlled by a steel wire.
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 9
With type C blades, lightning attachment points are predominantly found within a few tens of centimetres from the outermost tip of the blade, or on the sides of the tip at the position of the outermost end of the tip shaft.With type C as with type B blades, a lightning arc formed inside the tip section between the attachment point and the outermost end of the shaft causes severe damage.
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 10 | (Clause, Clause C.6)
On type C blades, damage to the main blade is mostly seen when the steel wire has been unable to carry the lightning current.Steel wires used for this purpose are of a minimum diameter of  $10\mathrm{mm}$  or  $12\mathrm{mm}$  for  $17\mathrm{m}$  long blades.Such wires are capable of conducting most lightning currents, and thereby protecting the main blade from damage (see Clause C.6 for further discussion on dimensioning of protection systems).
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 11
Type D is a blade constructed entirely from non-conducting materials.Experiences with non-conducting blades are that, as with the other types of blades, lightning attachment points are mostly found close to the tip.Compared to the other types of blades, attachment points can also be found randomly distributed at other positions along the length of the blade.
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 12 | (CFC, E)
Type E is a blade where some of the structural components are replaced with carbon fibre composite (CFC), since it has desirable mechanical properties.Depending on the specific design, CFC can be used as reinforcement of the blade skin, as well as for load carrying structural components, as the centre spar and main laminate.Due to its electrical properties, it might be integrated into the lightning protection system forming parts of the down
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 13
conductor.
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 14 | (CFC, Clause C.3.Lightning)
The issues of lightning protection of wind turbine blades containing CFC are treated in Clause C.3.Lightning flashes attaching to non-conducting blades or to insulating parts of blades containing conducting parts may at least partly be explained by the fact that pollution and water make such blades more conductive over time.High-voltage laboratory experiments have shown that arc attachments occur to a non-conducting blade sprayed with saline water practically as if the blade was metallic [9].
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 15 | (CFC)
Another part of the explanation is that the blades are simply in the way of lightning striking the wind turbine.In addition, it is known that discharges develop along a surface more easily than through air, and especially if the surface is contaminated with saline pollution and water.In any case, practical experience shows that severe lightning damage to both non-conducting blades (type D) and blades containing CFC (type E) is quite common and hence lightning protection is needed.
----------
# C.1.1 Types of blades and types of protection methods for blades|Part 16 | (Bibliography., Annex)
NOTE References to the literature are numbered [X] in this Annex and are listed in the Bibliography.
----------
# C.1.2 Blade damage mechanism|Part 1
Typical types of damage at the lightning attachment points are delamination and incineration of the surface composite material, and heating or melting of metallic components serving as the attachment point.The most severe damage to wind turbine blades occurs, however, when lightning forms high energy arcs inside the blade due to attachment to an unprotected part of the blade skin.The arcs may form in the air volume inside the blade or along the internal surfaces.
----------
# C.1.2 Blade damage mechanism|Part 2
Another type of damage is seen when the lightning current or part of it is conducted in or between layers of composite materials or in glue cracks in connection with the down conductor system, presumably because such layers and cracks hold some moisture.The pressure shock wave caused by such internal arcs may literally explode the blade, ripping the blade surface skins apart along the edges and from the internal carrying spar.
----------
# C.1.2 Blade damage mechanism|Part 3
All grades of damage are seen ranging from surface cracking to complete disintegration of the blade.In some cases, pressure waves have propagated from the blade struck by lightning through the hub and into the other blades causing pressure damage to them.Internal arcs often form between the lightning attachment point at the tip of the blade and some conducting component internal to the blade.
----------
# C.1.2 Blade damage mechanism|Part 4
With type C, the damage is often limited to the tip section, whereas the main blade is unharmed.Damage to type C main blades has normally been seen when an arc has formed inside the main blade.
----------
# C.1.2 Blade damage mechanism|Part 5
Typically, this has happened in cases where the steel wire controlling the tip brake was of insufficient cross section to conduct the lightning current from the tip shaft to the hub.
----------
# C.1.2 Blade damage mechanism|Part 6
With type A blades, the main blade is destroyed.
----------
# C.1.2 Blade damage mechanism|Part 7
The phenomenon responsible for the severe structural damage to wind turbine blades is therefore the formation of a pressure shock wave around an arc of lightning inside the blade.Minor damage may occur when a lightning arc is formed on the outside surface or when the lightning current is conducted by metallic components with insufficient cross section.
----------
# C.1.2 Blade damage mechanism|Part 8 | (C.2.4.)
The high energy internal arcing responsible for the structural damage is not to be confused with the low energy partial discharges commented in C.2.4.
----------
# C.2.1 General|Part 1
The generic problem of lightning protection of wind turbine blades is to conduct the lightning current safely from the attachment point to the hub, in such a way that the formation of a lightning arc inside the blade is avoided.This can be achieved by diverting the lightning current from the attachment point along the surface to the blade root, using metallic conductors either fixed to the blade surface or inside the blade.Another method is to add
----------
# C.2.1 General|Part 2
conducting material to the blade surface material itself, thus making the blade sufficiently conducting to carry the lightning current safely to the blade root.Variations of both these methods are used with wind turbine blades (see Figure C.2).
----------
# C.2.1 General|Part 3
![](01K8WFQ2YT/images/83af685ff9fc8cb6c6d5b99a1348e955f904f23c4b63482bc6d5707940c84be7.jpg)
----------
# C.2.1 General|Part 4
Figure C.2 - Lightning protection concepts for large modern wind turbine blades
----------
# C.2.2 Lightning air-termination systems on the blade surface or embedded in the surface|Part 1
Metallic conductors on the blade surface serving as an air-termination system or a down conductor system must have sufficient cross section to be able to withstand a direct lightning attachment and conduct the full lightning current.
----------
# C.2.2 Lightning air-termination systems on the blade surface or embedded in the surface|Part 2
In addition, certain dimensions are needed in order to achieve reliable fixing to the blade surface.The minimum cross section for aluminium is  $50~\mathrm{mm}^2$  and achieving reliable fixing of such conductors may be problematic.Furthermore, conductors mounted on the blade surface may compromise the aerodynamics of the blade or generate undesirable noise [10] [11].
----------
# C.2.2 Lightning air-termination systems on the blade surface or embedded in the surface|Part 3
For lightning conductors embedded in the blade, wires or braids of either aluminium or copper are used.In the literature, several protection systems are described where a metallic conductor connected to the blade root is placed either on the blade surface along the trailing edge of the blade or embedded in the trailing edge.Some blade designs have metallic conductors placed along both the leading and the trailing edge (type C).
----------
# C.2.2 Lightning air-termination systems on the blade surface or embedded in the surface|Part 4
In addition, some have metallic diverters placed on the surface around the blade at several positions along the blade, each of these being connected to conductors placed along the blade edges [11] [12] [13] [14] [15].
----------
# C.2.3 Adhesive metallic tapes and segmented diverter strips|Part 1
Adhesive aluminium tape placed on the blade surface has been used in several investigations.However, such tapes tended to peel off within a few months [12] [16].Provided that the problem of keeping the tape on the blade can be solved, it is possible that metallic tapes can be an interesting protection method, especially as a retrofit for existing unprotected
----------
# C.2.3 Adhesive metallic tapes and segmented diverter strips|Part 2
blades.
----------
# C.2.3 Adhesive metallic tapes and segmented diverter strips|Part 3
It should, however, be noted that large pressure waves are associated with guiding the flash close to the blade surface [10].This may lead to structural damage.Some promising experiments with segmented diverter strips have been performed in the past [17] [18].Such segmented strips are used on aircraft radomes because they do not interfere with the radar signal.
----------
# C.2.3 Adhesive metallic tapes and segmented diverter strips|Part 4 | (CFC)
The use of long-lasting segmented diverter strips as part of the lightning protection for a wind turbine blade containing CFC has been described in literature [26].It is possible that metallic tape can be used as one-shot protection requiring replacement after a lightning stroke.
----------
# C.2.4 Internal down conductor systems|Part 1
A solution to the problems with conductors placed on the blade surface is to have the lightning conductors placed inside the blade.Metallic fixtures for the conductor penetrate the blade surface and serve as discrete lightning receptors.Such protection systems are used on aircraft [10].The lightning protection system used on many blades currently in manufacture has discrete lightning receptors placed at the blade tip (types A and B in Figure C.2).
----------
# C.2.4 Internal down conductor systems|Part 2 | (C.2)
From the receptors at the tip, an internal down conductor system leads the lightning current to the blade root.For blades with tip brakes, the steel wire controlling the tip is used as a down conductor (type A).If the blade is without tip brake, then a copper wire placed along the internal spar is used as a down conductor (type B).Several thousands of blades with this lightning protection system (types A and B in Figure C.2) have been produced.
----------
# C.2.4 Internal down conductor systems|Part 3
The experiences with this lightning protection system for blades as long as  $20\mathrm{m}$  are very positive [19].The principle with one or more external air terminations connected to an internal down conductor has up till the date of publication been used widely by many manufacturers for blades up to  $60\mathrm{m}$ .For such long blades, experience has shown that there is a risk of direct lightning attachment through the laminate to the internal down conductor causing severe blade damage.
----------
# C.2.4 Internal down conductor systems|Part 4
These problems appear to be linked to uncontrolled partial discharges developing from the internal conductive parts (the down conductor, connection components, etc.
----------
# C.2.4 Internal down conductor systems|Part 5
).
----------
# C.2.4 Internal down conductor systems|Part 6
When such low-energy partial discharges are allowed to be inception from the interior metal parts of the blade, they will propagate equally fast as the ones inception from the receptors.Once these internal discharges strike the interior surface of the blade, they will, in connection with partial discharges on the blades' exterior, intensify the electrical stress experienced by the laminate.
----------
# C.2.4 Internal down conductor systems|Part 7 | (C.1.2.)
The increased stress might not be a problem for a limited number of rapid field changes (lightning striking receptors or nearby structures), but when the blade is exposed to several impacts during its entire service lifetime, the stress might eventually develop into a complete electrical breakdown.The physical impact on the blade from such a high voltage breakdown channel is rather limited, but the damage associated with the following lightning current will be disastrous as commented in C.1.2.
----------
# C.2.4 Internal down conductor systems|Part 8
Such discharges may be impeded or delayed by encapsulating the internal down conductor and other conductive parts in the blade with electrically insulating material, thereby reducing the problem [27] [28].
----------
# C.2.5 Conducting surface materials|Part 1
An alternative to a lightning air-termination system placed on the blade surface is to make the surface itself conducting.In the aircraft industry, lightning protection of glass and carbon fibre composite material for wings and surfaces exposed to lightning is achieved by adding conducting material to the outer layers, thereby reducing damage to a small area at the attachment point.
----------
# C.2.5 Conducting surface materials|Part 2
The conducting material may be metal sprayed onto the surface, metal coated fibres in the outer layers of the composite material, metal wire woven into the outer layers of the composite material, or meshes of metal placed just beneath the surface [10] [15]
----------
# C.2.5 Conducting surface materials|Part 3
[21] and [20].
----------
# C.2.5 Conducting surface materials|Part 4 | (C.2)
Lightning protection of wind turbine blades has been made with metal mesh placed along the sides of the blades just under the gel coat (D on Figure C.2).Sometimes the extreme tip of the blade is either made of metal or covered with a metal sheet [12] [13] [14] [15] [22] and [23].
----------
# C.2.5 Conducting surface materials|Part 5
The advantage of using metal meshes or other thin conducting surfaces for lightning down conductors is that possible interior conductive elements (CFC) are shielded from the electric field, and hence direct lightning attachments.The inductive voltage drop along the length of the conductor associated with the high current gradient will be slightly reduced, an important effect considering the risk of side flashes.
----------
# C.2.5 Conducting surface materials|Part 6
However, the risk of getting direct lightning attachment to the edge of such thin geometries, and the possibility of uneven current distribution due to skin effects must also be considered.
----------
# C.3 CFC structural components|Part 1 | (CFC)
Carbon fibre composites (CFC) have been used for tip shafts for small blades and are now a commonly used material for reinforcement of large blades.The material is used either for the load carrying centre spar or directly in the blade skins due to its superior mechanical properties.
----------
# C.3 CFC structural components|Part 2 | (CFC)
The use of CFC for structural components is expected to increase even further as the size of blades increases.The main issue with CFC is how it reacts to the impact from the lightning current possibly entering and flowing within the material.Here two electrical properties of CFC make it remarkably different than isotropic conductor materials like metals, the DC conductivity and the degree of anisotropy.
----------
# C.3 CFC structural components|Part 3 | (CFC, d.c.)
The d.c. conductivity of CFC is typically assigned a value which is a 1000 times lower than that of aluminium, i.e.
----------
# C.3 CFC structural components|Part 4 | (CFC)
$3,5\cdot 10^{4}\mathrm{S / m}$ .This is an approximate value found for biaxial woven CFC plates used for small aircraft skins, measured parallel with the surface of the sample [25] [21].
----------
# C.3 CFC structural components|Part 5 | (CFC)
Depending on the actual construction and weaving technique, the conductivity of CFC exhibit a very high degree of anisotropy.For CFC coupons used for lightning tests in the avionics industry, the conductivity has been measured and varies within four orders of magnitude for different current directions [29].Therefore, the resistive heating of CFC when exposed to high current densities might be critical.
----------
# C.3 CFC structural components|Part 6 | (CFC)
Especially at the lightning attachment points where high currents enter a rather confined area, the temperature due to joule losses might exceed the evaporation temperature of the matrix (approximately  $200^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ ).When the matrix evaporates, the pressure from evolving gases can cause rupture and delamination of the CFC layers.The CFC may even incinerate, in particular at the lightning attachment point [21].
----------
# C.3 CFC structural components|Part 7 | (CFC, CFC)
Where CFC is used in aircrafts, it is considered mandatory that lightning protection is provided for CFC components that may be struck by lightning or may conduct lightning current [10].There are examples of CFC tip shafts for wind turbine blades having been damaged by lightning.
----------
# C.3 CFC structural components|Part 8 | (CFC)
Some laboratory experiments have also demonstrated problems with CFC shafts conducting lightning current [24].Laboratory tests of blades with CFC skin have shown surface delamination and incineration at the lightning attachment point [9] [26].
----------
# C.3 CFC structural components|Part 9 | (CFC, CFC)
Protection of the CFC surfaces against direct lightning attachments is therefore required, either by means of encapsulation by a sufficient layer of insulation material or shielding by external lightning capturing devices.Since the conductive CFC is most often a parallel path to the lightning current, relative to the down conductor, proper bonding between CFC and other conducting components must be made.
----------
# C.3 CFC structural components|Part 10 | (CFC)
For each specific blade design, it should be determined whether the spacing between equipotential bondings is small enough to avoid the development of critical voltages betweenthe CFC and the down conductor.Critical voltages in this context are voltages that can potentially puncture the insulation layer between the CFC and the down conductor, affecting the mechanical strength of the structure.
----------
# C.3 CFC structural components|Part 11 | (CFC)
Once the lightning current has been distributed over a wide cross sectional area of the CFC, such structures may be able to conduct lightning current without being damaged.
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 1 | (CFC, Clause C.2, Clause C.3.)
Conductive components in this clause cover all other conductive parts in the blade besides the receptors and down conductor system described in Clause C.2, and the possible CFC described in Clause C.3.
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 2
![](01K8WFQ2YT/images/fecc1640fde5f1a30ad89389c4606730d3e05576c040e7f6fb102b80497a4f5a.jpg)Equivalent circuit
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 3
Figure C.3 - Lightning induced voltages between lightning conductor or structure and sensor wiring
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 4 | (IEC)
IEC 1195/10
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 5
Wiring for sensors placed on or inside blades may be exposed to strong magnetic fields that can produce damaging voltages between the lightning conductor and other wiring in the blade, as illustrated in Figure C.3.
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 6
Such wiring should be avoided if possible.
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 7
If not, both sensors and wiring must be protected by appropriate equipotential bonding to the down conductor system and shielded or covered by the external lightning receptors.Having well exposed external lightning receptors directly outside of internal conducting components should protect the internal structures from direct lightning attachment.
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 8
Furthermore, the risk of getting partial discharges from internal wiring is minimised by covering the conductive wiring carefully with electrically insulating materials.Note that high currents and voltages may be induced in isolated conductor loops in the vicinity of the down conductor system.
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 9
Such surges can possibly result in internal sparking.
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 10
It is possible that designs which integrate electrical wiring associated with sensors, lights and other systems with the lightning protection system, including the lightning down conductor, may be the most successful in avoiding damage to these systems.Careful coordination of designs of all systems contained within a blade is essential for successful lightning protection of the blade and functioning of the systems contained within the blade.Metallic structural components within the blade, i.e.
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 11
weights, dampers, platforms, etc.must be treated similarly.
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 12
All conductive parts in the blade must be designed to minimise electric field enhancement and be connected by equipotential bonding to reduce the risk of internal discharges.As with the wiring, it is important that the external air terminations shield off the internal conductive components from the electric field, hence protecting these areas from direct lightning attachment.If other conductive components are located within the blade, i.e.
----------
# C.4 Particular concerns with conducting components|Part 13
blade tip navigation light, lightning sensors, condition monitoring equipment, etc., it must always be shielded by external lightning receptors thereby minimising the risk of direct lightning attachment to the structures.As described previously, the risk of internal discharges possibly leading to puncture of the blade skin can be minimised by encapsulating all internal conductive parts carefully in electrical insulation material.
----------
# C.5 Interception efficiency|Part 1
The interception efficiency is an issue with the lightning protection methods using discrete air-termination systems placed on the blade surface.Any air terminations and extensions of air terminations (solid conductors and segmented diverters on the surface) must be placed in such a way that the likelihood of lightning puncturing a non-conducting surface is reduced to an acceptable level.
----------
# C.5 Interception efficiency|Part 2
The placement of air terminations would be such that the flashover voltage along the blade non-conducting surface is smaller than the breakdown voltage of the blade skin.In practice, both the breakdown voltage of the blade skin and the surface flashover voltage will be difficult to establish, as variations due to different composite materials as well as influence of ageing, cracks, humidity and pollution must be expected.
----------
# C.5 Interception efficiency|Part 3
Furthermore, the interception efficiency of segmented diverters and of discrete receptors will be influenced by the presence of conducting materials inside the blade [10].For blades up to  $20\mathrm{m}$  long, receptors at the tip of the blade have proven to be adequate.
----------
# C.5 Interception efficiency|Part 4
Recent publication of lightning attachment distribution for  $39\mathrm{m}$  glass fibre blades shows that the majority of lightning flashes attaches to the tip region of the blade  $(88\%)$  whereas the remaining strikes attached to the receptor  $5\mathrm{m}$  inboard the tip [30].
----------
# C.5 Interception efficiency|Part 5
High-voltage strike attachment tests on test specimens representing the design are useful for revealing insufficient receptor protection.
----------
# C.5 Interception efficiency|Part 6
However, further studies are needed, particularly of the effects of wet, polluted and aged blades.
----------
# C.5 Interception efficiency|Part 7
Numerical methods used to determine likely attachment areas on blades and nacelles and estimates of the annual number of direct lightning flash attachments to certain structures are currently being developed [32] [33].Once the potential and use of these models are fully understood, they can be used to estimate the surfaces where lightning may possibly strike if these surfaces were conductive or had receptors.
----------
# C.5 Interception efficiency|Part 8
It is not likely, however, that numerical simulation methods can predict with certainty whether a non-conductive blade structure would be punctured, or establish the number and location(s) of receptors necessary to prevent punctures.This is due to the complexity of most blade structures, and the dynamics of multiple streamer origination and growth.
----------
# C.5 Interception efficiency|Part 9 | (Clause D.2, Clause)
Thus the numerical methods may become useful design tools, but high-voltage attachment tests, as described in Clause D.2, of candidate designs should be used to provide additional assurance of protection effectiveness.
----------
# C.6 Dimensioning of lightning protection systems|html_block
The materials used for lightning protection of wind turbine blades must be able to withstand the combined effects of the electric, thermal and electrodynamic stresses imposed by the lightning current. Nominal dimensions for materials used for air termination and down conductors are listed in Table C.1 (see also IEC 62305-3).
