# Monitoring systems

It is recommended that wind turbines are equipped with equipment to detect lightning strikes/monitor the current levels of such lightning strikes. The purpose of such systems is to:
----------
# Monitoring systems|bullet_list_open
- provide information to the operator on the level of lightning strikes that have affected the wind turbine and to play a part in operation and maintenance regimes;
- provide valuable data on the expected number of lightning strikes to tall wind turbines and to assess their magnitude/characteristics, aiding in future risk assessment processes.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 1
Various options for monitoring systems exist.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 2
A brief description of these options follows below.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 3
a) Wide area lightning detection systems
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 4
Many commercial systems allow the detection of lightning using antennae that detect the electromagnetic impulse produced by a lightning flash.These use multiple antennae to locate lightning flashes based on direction finding or time of arrival techniques.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 5
Data from these systems is generally available in real time.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 6
The data output will not normally allow exact lightning flash to be pinpointed as the accuracy of such systems can be limited to from a few hundred metres to a few kilometres (the accuracy depends on the relative location of the lightning flash to the antennae and its magnitude).Such a system is therefore only of real use in confirming whether damage to a wind turbine was caused by lightning.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 7
b) Local active lightning detection systemsSpecial systems, e.g.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 8
with sensors mounted on the tower of a wind turbine to trigger a lightning alarm based on magnetic field criteria.The antennae prevent remote lightning flashes from triggering a false alarm.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 9 | (SCADA)
Such systems can be connected to a SCADA system giving a useful indication of lightning strikes in real time.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 10
The systems may or may not give an indication of current waveform or magnitude, and if placed on the tower, the system would not give an indication of the location of the lightning strike to a wind turbine.It is, however, a good option for an operator who wishes to be proactive in monitoring wind turbines after a lightning storm.Other developers have produced sensors that allow current transducers to be fitted directly into blades or on other lightning down conductors.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 11
Through the use of a transducer such as a rogowski coil or a fibre optic based technique, these sensors can both provide an alarm functionality and collect valuable peak current/waveform data for use in future risk assessment studies.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 12
c) Local passive lightning detection systems
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 13
Peak current sensor (PCS) cards have a magnetic strip with a pre-defined field pattern.They are clamped to a down-conductor, and the pre-defined pattern is partially erased by the magnetic field of the current flowing through the wire.The higher the lightning current, the higher the magnetic field around the down conductor and the more of the pre-defined field pattern that is erased/distorted.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 14
This form of system typically claims to have a detection range of  $3\mathrm{kA}$  to  $120\mathrm{kA}$  with results deviating not more than  $\pm 2\mathrm{kA}$ .
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 15
The cards only record peak currents and have the capability of storing only one such reading.Thus, in the event of multiple lightning strokes, only the highest peak current among all the strokes is stored.
----------
# Monitoring systems|Part 16 | (SCADA, SCADA)
There is no time reference and they cannot be interfaced into a SCADA system or similar.
----------
# Guidelines for small wind turbines – Microgeneration|Part 1
This standard is developed for use with industrial-scale wind turbines.These can be typified by certain features: power generation capacity greater than  $100\mathrm{kW}$ , mounted on towers taller than  $30\mathrm{m}$ , with a nacelle housing the generator, control and converter systems and blades longer than  $10\mathrm{m}$ .Below this size, there is a class of wind turbine known as small-scale or microgeneration.
----------
# Guidelines for small wind turbines – Microgeneration|Part 2
These are typically designed for domestic or light industrial applications where the power will primarily be intended for use on-site.
----------
# Guidelines for small wind turbines – Microgeneration|Part 3
Although there may be an ability to export excess power to the local electrical grid, these wind turbines only generate at LV and never at the MV levels which industry-scale turbines generate.The environments for these two distinct types of wind generators are very different and therefore the requirements and guidelines for lightning protection will also be quite different.The issue of lightning protection must still be considered for small-scale wind turbines.
----------
# Guidelines for small wind turbines – Microgeneration|Part 4
The main issue is to provide transient protection of grid connection and communication and control system connections (if any), in order to ensure that the systems can continue to operate after being exposed to the high transient voltages and currents associated with lightning transients originating within the wind turbine.
----------
# Guidelines for small wind turbines – Microgeneration|Part 5
Direct lightning strikes to small-scale systems will be relatively rare, unless placed very high and exposed.
----------
# Guidelines for small wind turbines – Microgeneration|Part 6
However, the systems need to remain safe, both in terms of maintaining physical integrity and not causing damage to people or property if structures break off and also in terms of avoiding the fire hazard or damage to the electrical system to which the turbine is connected.
----------
# Guidelines for small wind turbines – Microgeneration|Part 7
Even though this standard does not cover lightning protection of small-scale wind turbines, some of the general principles and approaches can still be beneficial in avoiding the risks mentioned above.
----------
# Guidelines for small wind turbines – Microgeneration|Part 8 | (Annex, D)
Direct testing using high voltage and high current will be very instructive in helping to design the lightning protection system (see Annex D regarding test methods).Components such as blades, anemometers and the generator housing can be tested, and the electrical circuitry and control system can be tested for resistance to effects of transient current surges.
----------
# Guidelines for small wind turbines – Microgeneration|Part 9 | (SPD)
The ultimate lightning protection solution may incorporate a lightning rod reaching above the rotor and equipotential electrical bonding and some form of surge protection device (SPD), which should again be validated for effectiveness by testing.
----------
